  6. Source slide page 6 (extracted text): ELECTRONIC PRINCIPLES AND DEVICES – UE20EC101 UNIT - I: Numerical on Diode’s Current Equations Q. No 1. Calculate the thermal voltage when the temperature is 25°C Solution: k = 1.38 x10-23 Joule/Kelvin. Thermal voltage VT =k T/q q=1.6 × 10−19 coulomb Where k is the Boltzmann constant and q is the charge of electron. This can be reduced to VT = T/11600 because q/k=11600 Therefore, VT = 298/11600 = 0.0257V VT =25-09-202625.7mV 6 EV0000168 · EV0000169 · EV0000170
  7. Source slide page 7 (extracted text): ELECTRONIC PRINCIPLES AND DEVICES – UE20EC101 UNIT - I: Numerical on Diode’s Current Equations Q.No.2 Calculate the forward bias current of a Si diode when forward bias voltage of 0.4V is applied, the reverse saturation current is 1.17×10-9A and the thermal voltage is 25.2mV. Solution: where , Is = reverse saturation current η = ideality factor, VT = thermal voltage, VD = applied voltage Since in this question ideality factor is not mentioned it can be taken as one. Is = 1.17 x 10-9A, VT = VT =k T/q =0.0252V, η = 1, VD = 0.4V Therefore, ID= 1.17×10-9x(e0.4/0.0252 -1) 25-09-2026 ID= 9.156mA. 7 EV0000171 · EV0000172 · EV0000173

5Zener and Avalanche breakdown, Temperature effects

This lesson is indexed from the source video titled Zener and Avalanche breakdown, Temperature effects. Its complete timestamped transcript is preserved below in segment order.   2. Source slide page 2 (extracted text): ELECTRONIC PRINCIPLES AND DEVICES Topic- 4 Zener and Avalanche breakdown, Temperature effects Department of Electronics and Communication. 25-09-2026 2 EV0000192 · EV0000193 · EV0000194
  3. Source slide page 3 (extracted text): ELECTRONIC PRINCIPLES AND DEVICES Types of Diode Breakdown • Avalanche breakdown : In the reverse bias condition as the voltage increases the free charge carriers obtain velocity and associated kinetic energy and release additional carriers through collision with other atomic structures. • Hence Covalent bonds are broken and electron–hole are generated. • These charge carriers acquire energy from the applied potential and produce more and more free charge carriers. This cumulative process is called Avalanche multiplication25-09-2026 or ionization process. 3 EV0000195 · EV0000196 · EV0000197
  4. Source slide page 4 (extracted text): ELECTRONIC PRINCIPLES AND DEVICES Types of Diode Breakdown • Zener breakdown : Zener break down occurs when both P and N type material are heavily doped in a semiconductor diode. Hence the depletion layer region is narrow down. • When a small reverse bias voltage is applied across a diode a very strong electric fied is produced. • Due to this electric field covalent bond breaks and produces large number of free charge carriers. 25-09-2026 4 EV0000198 · EV0000199 · EV0000200
  5. Source slide page 5 (extracted text): ELECTRONIC PRINCIPLES AND DEVICES Types of Diode Breakdown This is observed in Normal diode having a Avalanche breakdown voltage VR greater than 8 Volts 25-09-2026 5 EV0000201 · EV0000202 · EV0000203
  6. Source slide page 6 (extracted text): ELECTRONIC PRINCIPLES AND DEVICES Temperature Effects on V-I Characteristics of a Diode • The Effect of variation in temperature across a semiconductor diode is observed both in the forward as well as in reverse characteristics. • Rise in temperature generates more electron-hole pair thus conductivity increases and thus increases in current. • PN junction diode parameters like reverse saturation current, bias current, reverse breakdown voltage and barrier voltage are dependent on temperature. • Increase in the temperature increases carrier concentration. As a result, knee voltage decreases & breakdown voltage increases while reverse saturation current increases. 25-09-2026 6 EV0000204 · EV0000205 · EV0000206
  7. Source slide page 7 (extracted text): ELECTRONIC PRINCIPLES AND DEVICES Temperature Effects on V-I Characteristics of a Diode • Under Forward Bias the Change in Temperature across the diode: Barrier voltage is dependent on temperature hence it decreases by 2.5mV/ °C rise in temperature for both germanium and silicon. • Under Reverse Bias the Change in Temperature across the diode: Reverse saturation current (IS) of diode increases with increase in the temperature. The rise is 7%/°C for both germanium & silicon and approximately doubles for every 10°C rise in temperature. • Reverse breakdown voltage also increases as the temperature (VR) increases 25-09-2026 7 EV0000207 · EV0000208 · EV0000209
  8. Source slide page 8 (extracted text): ELECTRONIC PRINCIPLES AND DEVICES Temperature Effects on A Semiconductor diode 1. The Knee voltage shifts to the left at a rate of 2.5mV/0C increase in temperature . 2. The reverse saturation current Is will double in magnitude for every 10°C increase in temperature. 25-09-2026 8 EV0000210 · EV0000211 · EV0000212

  10. Source slide page 10 (extracted text): ELECTRONIC PRINCIPLES AND DEVICES Temperature Effects: Numerical Q No 1. The knee voltage of a Si diode is 0.7V and its reverse Saturation current is 20nA at 25oC. Determine these values at 40oC. Solution: . Knee voltage shift left at a rate of 2.5mV per centigrade degree increase in temperature. Therefore for a Change in temperature is = 40oC-25oC =15oC Change in knee voltage will be =2.5mV X 15= 0.0375V Hence VK at 40oC = 0.7-0.0375= 0.6625 V, Reverse Saturation Current doubles for every 10oC rise in Temperature IS at 40oC = 2(40-25/10) X 20nA = 2.82X20nA = 56.56nA. 25-09-2026 10 EV0000216 · EV0000217 · EV0000218

  16. Source slide page 16 (extracted text): ELECTRONIC PRINCIPLES AND DEVICES Simplified Diode Characteristics • Under forward-bias, the diode turns ON when a voltage of VK or VB is applied across it. It turns off if the applied voltage is less than VK. Thus, the diode is represented by a voltage of VK in series with a closed switch. Therefore, Vd = VB • Under reverse bias, it is an open switch. Note VB = Knee voltage 25-09-2026 16 EV0000282 · EV0000283 · EV0000284
  17. Source slide page 17 (extracted text): ELECTRONIC PRINCIPLES AND DEVICES Piece-Wise Linear Diode Characteristics • Under forward – bias, the diode behaves as a practical voltage source with the magnitude equal to the knee voltage in series with the the diodes average ac resistance. • Under reverse – bias, the diode is an open switch and no current flows through it. • Characteristics resemble closely to that of practical diode. • The voltage drop across the diode is calculated using the formula Vd = VB + Id *RB Note VB = Knee voltage 25-09-2026 17 EV0000285 · EV0000286 · EV0000287

Source slide page 3 (extracted text): ELECTRONIC PRINCIPLES AND DEVICES Ideal and Practical Diode Characteristics: Ideal Diode: When an ideal diode is forward biased, it offers no resistance & acts like a closed switch. Likewise, the ideal diode under reverse bias offers infinite resistance hence, it acts like open switch. Practical Diode: A diode which is said to be forward biased it starts conducting at knee voltage & under reverse bias no current due to majority charges hence a practical diode is considered to be open switch (minority25-09-2026 charges current ignored). For Si, VK = 0.7V 3 EV0000297 · EV0000298 · EV0000299

  3. Source slide page 3 (extracted text): ELECTRONIC PRINCIPLES AND DEVICES Clampers • A clamper is a circuit with a diode, resistor, and capacitor that shifts a waveform to a new DC level without altering its shape. • Also known as DC restorer. • Clamping networks have a capacitor connected directly from input Fig.1: Clamper • Resistive element connected in parallel with the output signal. • The diode is also in parallel with the output signal but may or may not have a series dc supply as an added element. 25-09-2026 3 EV0000465 · EV0000466 · EV0000467
  4. Source slide page 4 (extracted text): ELECTRONIC PRINCIPLES AND DEVICES Steps used for analysis: Step 1: Examine the response of the portion of the input signal that will forward bias the diode. • For Fig. 2, the diode is forward biased during the positive portion of the signal. • The short-circuit equivalent for the diode results in Vo = 0 V for t = 0 to T/2 . Step 2: During the on period of the diode, assume that the capacitor will charge up instantaneously to a voltage Fig.2 Fig.3 level determined by the surrounding network. Step 3: Assume the capacitor retains its voltage while the diode is "off." Step 4: Track the position and polarity of v o to ensure correct levels. • When the input turns negative, the network appears as in Fig. 3, with the diode as an open circuit and voltage stored across the capacitor. Step 5: Check that the total swing of the output matches that of the input. (see Fig. 4) Fig. 4 25-09-2026 4 EV0000468 · EV0000469 · EV0000470
  5.
